    "newString": "Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Evaluation framework and metric dissociation.}\n    We compare five confidence signals from three families---structural, surface, and model-internal---across quality estimation, binary discrimination, and sample selection.\n    These metrics decouple: EM leads AUROC (0.749) but ranks third in $\\rho$ (0.295), demonstrating that single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Diagnosing why selection fails beyond definitional limitations.}\n    Four of five signals are instance-level by definition and cannot rank candidates---this is not an empirical finding.\n    The empirical contribution is diagnosing why the two mechanisms that \\textit{could} bridge the gap both fail: (a)~consensus-proximity selection (MBR decoding) correlates with quality across instances but not within them; (b)~log-probability scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances within $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).\n    A controlled NER ablation confirms that LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), suggesting task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}\n    Signal rankings shift with task difficulty: EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard tasks---consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\n    At the capability boundary (WNUT-17, 52\\% zero F1), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance and LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---phenomena invisible on a single dataset that extend \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}'s quality-estimation analysis.\n    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).\n\\end{enumerate}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nSelf-consistency---sampling multiple outputs and aggregating them---has become a powerful strategy for improving language model reasoning.\nOn discrete-answer tasks such as arithmetic and commonsense QA, majority voting over chain-of-thought samples yields large gains: +18 percentage points on GSM8K (PaLM-540B; \\citealp{wang2023selfconsistency}).\nMore broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \\cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}.\nThese results rest on a shared assumption: that among $N$ stochastic samples, a selection mechanism can identify better outputs.\nFor tasks with a single correct answer, this assumption holds naturally---the majority vote is the selector.\nBut information extraction (IE) poses a fundamentally different challenge.\nThe output is not a single token or number; it is a \\textit{set} of entities or relations with internal dependencies, where two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical structures.\nThere is no obvious majority to vote on, partial agreement is the norm rather than the exception, and the space of valid outputs is combinatorially large.\nCan self-consistency still provide useful signal in this structured setting?\n\nPrior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated two distinct objectives: \\textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) and \\textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).\nThese require fundamentally different signal properties: the former benefits from instance-level aggregation, while the latter demands per-sample discrimination.\nSelf-consistency methods for discrete answers \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc} and weighted variants \\cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence---an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.\nThe most relevant study, \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}, examines consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based IE, finding results that our quality-estimation analysis corroborates.\nOur work extends theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings---WNUT-17 conditional correlations collapse to $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.) and LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---that would be invisible on a single dataset.\n\nStructured IE requires schema-compliant output, making constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided} the standard inference strategy---without it, a substantial fraction of outputs from general-purpose models are unparseable.\\footnote{Fine-tuned models may retain format compliance without grammar constraints: our free-form ablation achieves 99.95\\% valid JSON rate (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).}\nAll experiments use constrained decoding, the regime under which structured IE is practically deployed.\n\nOur central finding is the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 13--18~pp of oracle headroom.\nFour of five signals are instance-level by construction---this is definitional, not empirical.\nThe non-trivial questions are whether (a)~consensus-proximity selection, instantiating MBR decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high, muller2021understanding}, can recover per-sample discrimination, and (b)~whether log-probability, the only per-sample signal, can exploit the headroom.\nBoth fail: MBR utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); log-probability scores are severely compressed (60\\% of instances effectively tied), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).\nA controlled free-form decoding ablation confirms that this compression is intrinsic to the model and task, not an artifact of constrained decoding (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview}\n\\caption{The correlation-selection gap. \\textbf{Left}: high inter-sample agreement correlates with high F1 (quality estimation). \\textbf{Right}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all candidates, precluding sample selection.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe systematically compare five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting confidence [VC], Soft Jaccard [SJ]), surface (exact match [EM], Fleiss' $\\kappa$ [FK]), and model-internal (log-probability [LP])---across NER and RE on SciERC \\cite{luan2018scierc}, CoNLL-2003 \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, and WNUT-17 \\cite{derczynski2017wnut}, using Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B.\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{A systematic evaluation framework.}\n    We compare five confidence signals from three families---structural, surface, and model-internal---across quality estimation, binary discrimination, and sample selection, showing that these metrics decouple and single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{The correlation-selection gap and its diagnosis.}\n    Strong quality estimation ($\\rho{=}0.38$, AUROC${=}0.75$) coexists with selection failure ($p{>}0.39$, 13--18~pp oracle headroom).\n    The instance-level failure of four signals is definitional; the empirical contributions are diagnosing why consensus-proximity selection (MBR decoding) and log-probability both fail.\n    LP score compression (60\\% of instances effectively tied) is the primary bottleneck; where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).\n    A controlled ablation confirms that compression is intrinsic, not decoding-regime-specific.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}\n    Signal rankings shift with task difficulty: EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard tasks---consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\n    At the capability boundary (WNUT-17, 52\\% zero F1), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance and LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---phenomena invisible on a single dataset that extend \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}'s quality-estimation analysis.\n    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HoTVNDaRoAwoGcWh1ZAGPJ`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}
4	For per-instance quality ranking, VC provides the strongest rank correlation ($\rho{=}0.38$), though EM leads binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.749$); under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).
5	Signal rankings depend on task difficulty---EM dominates easy tasks (CoNLL), VC and SJ dominate hard tasks (SciERC)---directionally consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.
6	At higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).
7	When model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17), all signals converge toward non-significant conditional correlations (Table~\ref{tab:wnut}).
8	
9	\paragraph{Why the gap exists: two layers.}
10	\textit{Layer 1 (definitional):} Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.
11	Consensus-proximity selection (\S\ref{sec:evaluation}) does not recover per-sample discrimination (Table~\ref{tab:selection}).
12	\textit{Layer 2 (score compression):} Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, also fails to exploit the 13--18~pp oracle headroom.
13	LP scores are severely compressed: 60\% of instances have a within-sample range below 0.05, and 75\% of pairwise comparisons are effective ties ($|\Delta|{<}0.026$; Appendix~\ref{app:logprob_ties}).
14	In the 40\% of instances where compression is mitigated (LP range ${\geq}0.05$, $n{=}210$), LP \textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$; within-instance $\rho$: 0.08$\to$0.39).
15	This diagnostic confirms that LP carries useful per-sample quality information; score compression is the primary mechanistic bottleneck.
16	A controlled ablation (\S\ref{sec:ablation}) confirms that this compression is intrinsic to the model and task, not an artifact of constrained decoding.
17	Diversity-stratified analysis confirms the gap persists even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}); RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339, preliminary; Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}) may compound this.
18	
19	\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}
20	Our selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\hat{y} = \arg\max_{y_i} \sum_{j \neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.
21	Despite MBR's success in machine translation \cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here because the utility functions correlate with F1 at the instance level but not within a single instance---the mathematical essence of the correlation-selection gap.
22	\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs---consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.
23	
24	\paragraph{Score compression is task-inherent.}\label{sec:ablation}
25	A natural hypothesis is that LP compression is an artifact of constrained decoding's restricted token space.
26	We test this via controlled ablation: generating free-form (unconstrained) outputs on the same instances with post-hoc JSON parsing (parse rate 99.95\%, 4406/4408; Table~\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}).
27	LP variance is essentially unchanged (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\times}$, tied fraction 59.7\%$\to$57.7\%).
28	NER selection gains remain negligible ($+0.18$~pp free-form vs.\ $+0.59$~pp constrained).
29	This rules out constrained decoding as the cause of compression and establishes LP compression as inherent to structured output generation.
30	The correlation-selection gap is therefore a fundamental challenge for test-time scaling in structured IE, not an artifact of any particular decoding strategy.
31	Notably, the fine-tuned model achieved 99.95\% parse rate without grammar constraints, suggesting that constrained decoding's format-compliance role may be redundant for sufficiently fine-tuned models.
32	
33	\paragraph{What drives the universal gap?}
34	Our analysis reveals two complementary mechanisms.
35	First, the free-form ablation exposes a task-specific interaction: RE LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that is universal across all 7 relation types and driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).
36	The win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).
37	This interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.
38	This ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); multi-seed replication is left for future work.
39	Second, the non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472, indicating that 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, yielding a $3{\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.
40	Despite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible---score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.
41	Free-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\rho$: 0.379$\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.
42	
43	\paragraph{Methodological implications.}
44	The decoupling of quality estimation ($\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.
45	Effective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:additional}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.
46	Cross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\ $0.386$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\ref{app:ood}).
47	Our findings are established under constrained decoding with a single-dataset ablation suggesting the gap is intrinsic; whether this generalizes across decoding regimes, datasets, and model scales warrants further investigation.
48	
```

    "oldString": "\\paragraph{Score compression is task-inherent.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nA natural hypothesis is that LP compression is an artifact of constrained decoding's restricted token space.\nWe test this via controlled ablation: generating free-form (unconstrained) outputs on the same instances with post-hoc JSON parsing (parse rate 99.95\\%, 4406/4408; Table~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}).\nLP variance is essentially unchanged (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$, tied fraction 59.7\\%$\\to$57.7\\%).\nNER selection gains remain negligible ($+0.18$~pp free-form vs.\\ $+0.59$~pp constrained).\nThis rules out constrained decoding as the cause of compression and establishes LP compression as inherent to structured output generation.\nThe correlation-selection gap is therefore a fundamental challenge for test-time scaling in structured IE, not an artifact of any particular decoding strategy.\nNotably, the fine-tuned model achieved 99.95\\% parse rate without grammar constraints, suggesting that constrained decoding's format-compliance role may be redundant for sufficiently fine-tuned models.",
    "newString": "\\paragraph{Score compression under free-form decoding.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nA natural hypothesis is that LP compression is an artifact of constrained decoding's restricted token space.\nWe test this via controlled ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, single dataset): generating free-form (unconstrained) outputs on the same instances with post-hoc JSON parsing (parse rate 99.95\\%, 4406/4408; Table~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}).\nFor NER, LP variance is essentially unchanged (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$, tied fraction 59.7\\%$\\to$57.7\\%) and selection gains remain negligible ($+0.18$~pp free-form vs.\\ $+0.59$~pp constrained), establishing that NER LP compression persists independent of decoding regime in our setting.\nHowever, the ablation reveals a task-dependent interaction: RE LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single seed), suggesting that constrained decoding may be a contributing factor for RE even though it is not for NER.\nThe NER/RE divergence indicates that the relationship between decoding regime and selection is task-specific rather than universal.\nNotably, the fine-tuned model achieved 99.95\\% parse rate without grammar constraints, suggesting that constrained decoding's format-compliance role may be redundant for sufficiently fine-tuned models.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}\nFor per-instance quality ranking, VC provides the strongest rank correlation ($\\rho{=}0.38$), though EM leads binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.749$); under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).\nSignal rankings depend on task difficulty---EM dominates easy tasks (CoNLL), VC and SJ dominate hard tasks (SciERC)---directionally consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\nAt higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).\nWhen model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17), all signals converge toward non-significant conditional correlations (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why the gap exists: two layers.}\n\\textit{Layer 1 (definitional):} Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.\nConsensus-proximity selection (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}) does not recover per-sample discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}).\n\\textit{Layer 2 (score compression):} Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, also fails to exploit the 13--18~pp oracle headroom.\nLP scores are severely compressed: 60\\% of instances have a within-sample range below 0.05, and 75\\% of pairwise comparisons are effective ties ($|\\Delta|{<}0.026$; Appendix~\\ref{app:logprob_ties}).\nIn the 40\\% of instances where compression is mitigated (LP range ${\\geq}0.05$, $n{=}210$), LP \\textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$; within-instance $\\rho$: 0.08$\\to$0.39).\nThis diagnostic confirms that LP carries useful per-sample quality information; score compression is the primary mechanistic bottleneck.\nA controlled ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}) confirms that this compression is intrinsic to the model and task, not an artifact of constrained decoding.\nDiversity-stratified analysis confirms the gap persists even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}); RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339, preliminary; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) may compound this.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}\nOur selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\\hat{y} = \\arg\\max_{y_i} \\sum_{j \\neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.\nDespite MBR's success in machine translation \\cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \\cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here because the utility functions correlate with F1 at the instance level but not within a single instance---the mathematical essence of the correlation-selection gap.\n\\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs---consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.\n\n\\paragraph{Score compression is task-inherent.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nA natural hypothesis is that LP compression is an artifact of constrained decoding's restricted token space.\nWe test this via controlled ablation: generating free-form (unconstrained) outputs on the same instances with post-hoc JSON parsing (parse rate 99.95\\%, 4406/4408; Table~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}).\nLP variance is essentially unchanged (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$, tied fraction 59.7\\%$\\to$57.7\\%).\nNER selection gains remain negligible ($+0.18$~pp free-form vs.\\ $+0.59$~pp constrained).\nThis rules out constrained decoding as the cause of compression and establishes LP compression as inherent to structured output generation.\nThe correlation-selection gap is therefore a fundamental challenge for test-time scaling in structured IE, not an artifact of any particular decoding strategy.\nNotably, the fine-tuned model achieved 99.95\\% parse rate without grammar constraints, suggesting that constrained decoding's format-compliance role may be redundant for sufficiently fine-tuned models.\n\n\\paragraph{What drives the universal gap?}\nOur analysis reveals two complementary mechanisms.\nFirst, the free-form ablation exposes a task-specific interaction: RE LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that is universal across all 7 relation types and driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).\nThe win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).\nThis interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.\nThis ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); multi-seed replication is left for future work.\nSecond, the non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472, indicating that 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, yielding a $3{\\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.\nDespite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible---score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.\nFree-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\\rho$: 0.379$\\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.\nCross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.386$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nOur findings are established under constrained decoding with a single-dataset ablation suggesting the gap is intrinsic; whether this generalizes across decoding regimes, datasets, and model scales warrants further investigation.\n",

multi-seed replication is left for future work.\nSecond, the non-tied subset ($n{=}210$) is partially reshuffled under free-form decoding: Jaccard similarity between the constrained and free-form non-tied sets is 0.472, indicating that 87 instances newly gain LP discrimination while others lose it, yielding a $3{\\times}$ increase in within-instance LP range for the newly non-tied subset.\nDespite this reshuffling, aggregate NER selection gains remain negligible---score compression is not a fixed property of specific instances but rather a pervasive regime that shifts across instances without producing net selection improvement.\nFree-form decoding also slightly improves quality estimation (NER VC $\\rho$: 0.379$\\to$0.409), though this does not translate to selection gains.\n\n\\paragraph{Methodological implications.}\nThe decoupling of quality estimation ($\\rho$), binary discrimination (AUROC), and sample selection (best-of-$N$ F1) means evaluating on a single metric risks selecting the wrong signal for the intended use case.\nEffective selection likely requires per-sample signals such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers, venktesh2025trust}; $N$-scaling analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}) shows $N{=}8$ captures most quality estimation benefit.\nCross-dataset evaluation confirms that structural signals are robust to domain shift when the training schema subsumes the target (VC $\\rho{=}0.469$ OOD vs.\\ $0.386$ in-domain); the reverse direction fails due to catastrophic model failure, not signal failure (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}).\nOur findings are established under constrained decoding with a single-dataset ablation suggesting the gap is intrinsic; whether this generalizes across decoding regimes, datasets, and model scales warrants further investigation.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Practical recommendations.}\nFor per-instance quality ranking, VC provides the strongest rank correlation ($\\rho{=}0.38$), though EM leads binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.749$); under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).\nSignal rankings depend on task difficulty---EM dominates easy tasks (CoNLL), VC and SJ dominate hard tasks (SciERC)---directionally consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\nAt higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).\nWhen model capability is insufficient (WNUT-17), all signals converge toward non-significant conditional correlations (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}).\n\n\\paragraph{Why the gap exists: two layers.}\n\\textit{Layer 1 (definitional):} Four of five signals are instance-level aggregates that assign a single score per input; they cannot rank candidates by construction.\nConsensus-proximity selection (\\S\\ref{sec:evaluation}) does not recover per-sample discrimination (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}).\n\\textit{Layer 2 (score compression):} Log-probability, the only per-sample signal, also fails to exploit the 13--18~pp oracle headroom.\nLP scores are severely compressed: 60\\% of instances have a within-sample range below 0.05, and 75\\% of pairwise comparisons are effective ties ($|\\Delta|{<}0.026$; Appendix~\\ref{app:logprob_ties}).\nIn the 40\\% of instances where compression is mitigated (LP range ${\\geq}0.05$, $n{=}210$), LP \\textit{significantly} outperforms greedy ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$; within-instance $\\rho$: 0.08$\\to$0.39).\nThis diagnostic confirms that LP carries useful per-sample quality information; score compression is the primary mechanistic bottleneck.\nA controlled ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}) confirms that this compression is intrinsic to the model and task, not an artifact of constrained decoding.\nDiversity-stratified analysis confirms the gap persists even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity}); RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339, preliminary; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) may compound this.\n\n\\paragraph{Connection to MBR decoding.}\nOur selection strategies instantiate minimum Bayes risk (MBR) decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, muller2021understanding}: each selects $\\hat{y} = \\arg\\max_{y_i} \\sum_{j \\neq i} U(y_i, y_j)$ with utility $U$ defined by SJ, EM, or entity overlap.\nDespite MBR's success in machine translation \\cite{freitag2022high} and its extension to summarization, code generation, and captioning \\cite{jinnai2024mbr}, all variants fail here because the utility functions correlate with F1 at the instance level but not within a single instance---the mathematical essence of the correlation-selection gap.\n\\citet{eikema2025structured} study structure-conditional MBR decoding and find that standard utility functions degrade on structured outputs---consistent with our finding that consensus-proximity selection fails in IE despite its success in machine translation.\n\n\\paragraph{Score compression under free-form decoding.}\\label{sec:ablation}\nA natural hypothesis is that LP compression is an artifact of constrained decoding's restricted token space.\nWe test this via controlled ablation on SciERC (Qwen3-8B, single dataset): generating free-form (unconstrained) outputs on the same instances with post-hoc JSON parsing (parse rate 99.95\\%, 4406/4408; Table~\\ref{tab:freeform_ablation}).\nFor NER, LP variance is essentially unchanged (within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$, tied fraction 59.7\\%$\\to$57.7\\%) and selection gains remain negligible ($+0.18$~pp free-form vs.\\ $+0.59$~pp constrained), establishing that NER LP compression persists independent of decoding regime in our setting.\nHowever, the ablation reveals a task-dependent interaction: RE LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single seed), suggesting that constrained decoding may be a contributing factor for RE even though it is not for NER.\nThe NER/RE divergence indicates that the relationship between decoding regime and selection is task-specific rather than universal.\nNotably, the fine-tuned model achieved 99.95\\% parse rate without grammar constraints, suggesting that constrained decoding's format-compliance role may be redundant for sufficiently fine-tuned models.\n\n\\paragraph{Task-specific decoding interactions.}\nThe free-form ablation reveals divergent behavior across tasks.\nOn RE, LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that spans all 7 relation types and is driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).\nThe win/loss ratio shifts from 36:59 (constrained) to 45:45 (free-form), with the gains concentrated on relation-dense instances (mean 3.3 gold relations).\nThis interaction does not generalize to NER, where selection remains negligible under both regimes.\nThis ablation uses a single seed (seed${=}42$); ```text
22	Our central finding is the \textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 13--18~pp of oracle headroom.
23	Four of five signals are instance-level by construction---this is definitional, not empirical.
24	The non-trivial questions are whether (a)~consensus-proximity selection, instantiating MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high, muller2021understanding}, can recover per-sample discrimination, and (b)~whether log-probability, the only per-sample signal, can exploit the headroom.
25	Both fail: MBR utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (\S\ref{sec:discussion}); log-probability scores are severely compressed (60\% of instances effectively tied), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).
26	A controlled free-form decoding ablation confirms that this compression is intrinsic to the model and task, not an artifact of constrained decoding (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
27	
28	\begin{figure*}[t]

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nSelf-consistency---sampling multiple outputs and aggregating them---has become a powerful strategy for improving language model reasoning.\nOn discrete-answer tasks such as arithmetic and commonsense QA, majority voting over chain-of-thought samples yields large gains: +18 percentage points on GSM8K (PaLM-540B; \\citealp{wang2023selfconsistency}).\nMore broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \\cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}.\nThese results rest on a shared assumption: that among $N$ stochastic samples, a selection mechanism can identify better outputs.\nFor tasks with a single correct answer, this assumption holds naturally---the majority vote is the selector.\nBut information extraction (IE) poses a fundamentally different challenge.\nThe output is not a single token or number; it is a \\textit{set} of entities or relations with internal dependencies, where two samples may extract overlapping but non-identical structures.\nThere is no obvious majority to vote on, partial agreement is the norm rather than the exception, and the space of valid outputs is combinatorially large.\nCan self-consistency still provide useful signal in this structured setting?\n\nPrior work on confidence estimation for LLM-based IE has conflated two distinct objectives: \\textit{quality estimation} (which inputs does the model handle reliably?) and \\textit{sample selection} (which of $N$ outputs is best?).\nThese require fundamentally different signal properties: the former benefits from instance-level aggregation, while the latter demands per-sample discrimination.\nSelf-consistency methods for discrete answers \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency, chen2024usc} and weighted variants \\cite{taubenfeld2025cisc, kang2025selfcertainty} assume outputs can be grouped by equivalence---an assumption that breaks for entity sets with partial overlap.\nThe most relevant study, \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}, examines consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based IE, finding results that our quality-estimation analysis corroborates.\nOur work extends theirs in three directions: (i)~structural matching beyond surface-level agreement, (ii)~testing whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection (it does not), and (iii)~a difficulty gradient revealing non-obvious findings---WNUT-17 conditional correlations collapse to $\\rho{=}0.038$ (n.s.) and LP exhibits strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---that would be invisible on a single dataset.\n\nStructured IE requires schema-compliant output, making constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided} the standard inference strategy---without it, a substantial fraction of outputs from general-purpose models are unparseable.\\footnote{Fine-tuned models may retain format compliance without grammar constraints: our free-form ablation achieves 99.95\\% valid JSON rate (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).}\nAll experiments use constrained decoding, the regime under which structured IE is practically deployed.\n\nOur central finding is the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 13--18~pp of oracle headroom.\nFour of five signals are instance-level by construction---this is definitional, not empirical.\nThe non-trivial questions are whether (a)~consensus-proximity selection, instantiating MBR decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high, muller2021understanding}, can recover per-sample discrimination, and (b)~whether log-probability, the only per-sample signal, can exploit the headroom.\nBoth fail: MBR utility correlates with F1 across instances but not within them (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); log-probability scores are severely compressed (60\\% of instances effectively tied), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).\nA controlled free-form decoding ablation confirms that this compression is intrinsic to the model and task, not an artifact of constrained decoding (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_overview}\n\\caption{The correlation-selection gap. \\textbf{Left}: high inter-sample agreement correlates with high F1 (quality estimation). \\textbf{Right}: instance-level signals assign identical scores to all candidates, precluding sample selection.}\n\\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nWe systematically compare five confidence signals from three families---structural (entity voting confidence [VC], Soft Jaccard [SJ]), surface (exact match [EM], Fleiss' $\\kappa$ [FK]), and model-internal (log-probability [LP])---across NER and RE on SciERC \\cite{luan2018scierc}, CoNLL-2003 \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll}, and WNUT-17 \\cite{derczynski2017wnut}, using Qwen3-8B and LLaMA-3.1-8B.\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Evaluation framework and metric dissociation.}\n    We compare five confidence signals from three families---structural, surface, and model-internal---across quality estimation, binary discrimination, and sample selection.\n    These metrics decouple: EM leads AUROC (0.749) but ranks third in $\\rho$ (0.295), demonstrating that single-metric evaluation risks selecting the wrong signal.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Diagnosing why selection fails beyond definitional limitations.}\n    Four of five signals are instance-level by definition and cannot rank candidates---this is not an empirical finding.\n    The empirical contribution is diagnosing why the two mechanisms that \\textit{could} bridge the gap both fail: (a)~consensus-proximity selection (MBR decoding) correlates with quality across instances but not within them; (b)~log-probability scores are compressed into effective ties (60\\% of instances within $\\varepsilon{=}0.05$), though where compression is mitigated, LP becomes a significant selector ($+2.9$~pp, $p{=}0.01$).\n    A controlled NER ablation confirms that LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), suggesting task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Task-complexity boundary conditions.}\n    Signal rankings shift with task difficulty: EM leads on easy tasks, SJ on hard tasks---consistent across two model families at 8B scale, though with model-dependent magnitude.\n    At the capability boundary (WNUT-17, 52\\% zero F1), conditional correlations collapse to non-significance and LP exhibits strong negative correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.410$)---phenomena invisible on a single dataset that extend \\citet{nedellec2026consistency}'s quality-estimation analysis.\n    NER findings span three datasets; RE conclusions rest on SciERC alone (preliminary).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\n",

the best candidate is a granularity limitation, not a sample size limitation.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:243:On CoNLL, diversity collapses: 57\\% (Qwen) to 88\\% (LLaMA) of instances produce identical outputs across all $N$ samples, reflecting a ceiling effect where greedy F1 already exceeds 0.90.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:306:On RE (Qwen only, preliminary), VC remains the top signal ($\\rho{=}0.309{\\pm}0.024$), but LP rises to \\#2 ($\\rho{=}0.297{\\pm}0.022$), above FK, SJ, and EM---a reversal from the NER ranking that reflects RE's higher sensitivity to per-sample scoring.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:322:\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{RE Spearman $\\rho$ (mean $\\pm\\sigma$, Qwen)}} \\\\\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:412:\\section{RE Conditional Selection}\\label{app:re_conditional_selection}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:414:Table~\\ref{tab:re_cond_selection} extends the selection analysis to the RE non-zero F1 subset.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:415:Of $n{=}397$ RE instances, 238 (60\\%) have non-zero F1 and 159 (40\\%) have zero F1.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:418:Oracle headroom exists (0.757 vs.\\ 0.674) but no signal exploits it, ruling out the 40\\% zero-F1 floor effect as the explanation for RE selection failure.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:423:\\caption{RE selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Full set ($n{=}397$) and non-zero F1 subset ($n{=}238$). No signal outperforms greedy on either subset.}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:445:LP score distributions are nearly identical across regimes (Panel~A: within-instance std ratio $0.97{\\times}$), and NER selection gains remain negligible under both regimes (Panel~B).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:446:RE LP selection flips positive under free-form decoding ($+1.69$~pp vs.\\ $-0.68$~pp constrained).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:527:We analyze RE LP selection failures across three random seeds (constrained decoding, $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, Qwen3-8B) to assess whether failures are systematic or stochastic.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:530:LP selection failures (instances where LP-selected F1 $<$ greedy F1) show moderate cross-seed stability: the average pairwise Jaccard similarity is 0.42 (range: 0.38--0.46), indicating that failures are partially systematic---driven by instance complexity---but not fully deterministic.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:535:Stable failures (all 3 seeds) involve more gold relations (2.12 vs.\\ 1.62 for never-fail instances) and higher greedy F1 (0.688 vs.\\ 0.429), indicating that LP mis-selects on instances where the model already performs well---precisely the instances where selection would be most valuable.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:541:\\caption{RE LP selection failure stability across 3 seeds (SciERC, $N{=}8$). Stable failures concentrate on relation-dense instances with high greedy F1.}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:557:These instances show large constrained LP errors ($\\Delta{=}{-}0.430$) that reverse under free-form decoding ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.503$), but their rarity (5/397) confirms that the RE selection flip is driven by aggregate variance reallocation rather than systematic instance-level correction.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:570: & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{NER} $\\rho$} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{RE} $\\rho$} \\\\\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:610: & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{NER} $\\sigma_\\rho$} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{RE} $\\sigma_\\rho$} \\\\\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex:658:The CoNLL-trained model recognizes only 4 news entity types (PER, ORG, LOC, MISC) and has no relation extraction capability; when applied to SciERC's 6 scientific entity types and 7 relation types, the model cannot produce meaningful structured outputs (greedy NER F1${=}0.009$, RE F1${=}0.001$).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:5:More broadly, test-time compute scaling through repeated sampling and selection has emerged as a complementary axis to model scaling, offering substantial quality improvements at inference time \\cite{snell2025scaling, brown2024monkeys}.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:22:Our central finding is the \\textbf{correlation-selection gap} (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}): consistency-based signals achieve strong quality estimation ($\\rho$ up to 0.38, AUROC up to 0.75) but fail at sample selection despite 13--18~pp of oracle headroom.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:24:The non-trivial questions are whether (a)~consensus-proximity selection, instantiating MBR decoding \\cite{eikema2022sampling, freitag2022high, muller2021understanding}, can recover per-sample discrimination, and (b)~whether log-probability, the only per-sample signal, can exploit the headroom.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:26:A controlled free-form decoding ablation confirms that NER LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), revealing task-dependent interaction with decoding regime (\\S\\ref{sec:ablation}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:39:    We compare five confidence signals from three families---structural, surface, and model-internal---across quality estimation, binary discrimination, and sample selection.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/docs/paper/sections/introduction.tex:45:    A controlled NER ablation confirms that LP compression persists without constrained decoding; on RE, LP selection reverses direction (preliminary), suggesting task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.",

### Tool result

_source line 2630 · 2026-05-15T12:45:42.302Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0139MJn6txkEjoAbrUVhVbGG`</summary>

```text
4:For per-instance quality ranking, VC provides the strongest rank correlation ($\rho{=}0.38$), though EM leads binary discrimination (AUROC${=}0.749$); under conditional filtering, EM leads on both NER and RE ($\rho{=}0.360$, $0.417$; RE: preliminary, single benchmark).
6:At higher sample sizes ($N{=}16$), SJ converges to comparable NER performance with VC ($\Delta{=}0.002$) with lower cross-seed variance ($\sigma{=}0.010$ vs.\ $0.026$); on RE, only VC consistently outperforms LP (preliminary).
17:Diversity-stratified analysis confirms the gap persists even among high-diversity instances (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity}); RE miscalibration (ECE 0.109--0.339, preliminary; Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}) may compound this.
28:However, the ablation reveals a task-dependent interaction: RE LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single seed), suggesting that constrained decoding may be a contributing factor for RE even though it is not for NER.
29:The NER/RE divergence indicates that the relationship between decoding regime and selection is task-specific rather than universal.
34:On RE, LP selection flips from $-0.68$~pp (constrained) to $+1.69$~pp (free-form; preliminary, single RE benchmark), a reversal that spans all 7 relation types and is driven by selective variance reallocation ($p{<}0.0001$).
46:Our findings are established under constrained decoding at 8B scale; a single-dataset NER ablation suggests LP compression persists without constrained decoding, but RE shows task-dependent interaction with decoding regime.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TKeckjXbBKFB1f3De5Leek`</summary>

```text
19	\paragraph{Uncertainty estimation and calibration.}
20	Surveys cover confidence estimation \cite{geng2024confidence}, selective abstention \cite{wen2025know}, uncertainty quantification \cite{xia2025survey}, and calibration \cite{hobelsberger2025systematic} for LLMs; \citet{xiong2024llm} find LLMs tend toward overconfidence, and \citet{heo2025uncertainty} show calibration degrades with task complexity.
21	Semantic uncertainty \cite{kuhn2023semantic} clusters generations by meaning; \citet{kossen2024semantic} distill this into probes; P(True) \cite{kadavath2022language} queries model confidence directly; \citet{malinin2021uncertainty} distinguish data and model uncertainty in autoregressive models.
22	Our structural signals instead measure agreement over output \textit{structure}.
23	Neural networks are often miscalibrated \cite{guo2017calibration, desai2020calibration}; deep ensembles \cite{lakshminarayanan2017ensembles} and MC-dropout \cite{gal2016dropout} obtain uncertainty from model variation, while our approach requires no model modification.
24	Prior NER uncertainty work spans Bayesian inference for sequence labeling \cite{xiao2019uncertainty}, active learning under uncertainty \cite{shelmanov2021active}, evidential deep learning \cite{zhang2023ener}, verification-augmented NER \cite{kim2024verifiner}, and uncertainty transmission across label sequences \cite{he2024uncertainty}---all within discriminative paradigms.
25	Conformal prediction \cite{fisch2021conformal, angelopoulos2021conformal} provides coverage guarantees; trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers, lightman2024verify, venktesh2025trust} and reward models \cite{wang2024mathshepherd} learn per-sample scoring.
26	Our selection strategies instantiate MBR decoding \cite{eikema2022sampling} with structural utility functions; despite MBR's success in translation \cite{freitag2022high} and other generation tasks \cite{jinnai2024mbr}, we find utility-quality misalignment at the per-sample level for structured IE (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
27	Selective prediction \cite{geifman2017selective} and confidence-based IE filtering \cite{culotta2004confidence} establish the broader framework.
28	
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Self-consistency and test-time scaling.}\nSelf-consistency decoding \\cite{wang2023selfconsistency} improves reasoning by majority voting over chain-of-thought samples, assuming a discrete answer space.\nExtensions to free-form text use LLM-based comparison \\cite{chen2024usc}, atomic sub-claim decomposition \\cite{thirukovalluru2024atomic}, or cross-sample hallucination detection \\cite{manakul2023selfcheckgpt}.\nFor test-time scaling \\cite{zhang2025tts}, \\citet{snell2025scaling} characterize compute scaling laws and \\citet{brown2024monkeys} show best-of-$N$ gains across tasks.\nRecent reward-free selectors include confidence-weighted voting \\cite{taubenfeld2025cisc}, KL-based self-certainty \\cite{kang2025selfcertainty}, adaptive sampling \\cite{kim2026reasc}, ranked aggregation \\cite{wang2025rankedvoting}, and latent-space consistency \\cite{oh2025latentsc}.\nConcurrently, \\citet{feng2025optimal} and \\citet{wang2025make} adapt self-consistency to difficulty-aware settings, echoing our finding that signal rankings shift with task complexity; \\citet{li2025llms} propose generative self-aggregation for IE but focus on output fusion rather than confidence-based selection.\nAll of the above target reasoning/QA with discrete answers or specific aggregation strategies.\nOur work extends self-consistency to set-valued structured outputs and identifies the correlation-selection gap: strong quality estimation ($\\rho{=}0.38$) coexists with selection failure ($p{>}0.39$).\n\n\\paragraph{Consistency for structured IE.}\n\\citet{nedellec2026consistency} study consistency--accuracy correlations for LLM-based NER/RE, corroborating our quality-estimation findings.\nTheir work leaves open three questions we address: (i)~whether structural matching improves on surface-level agreement (which they acknowledge ``overlooks semantically consistent outputs''); (ii)~whether quality estimation translates to best-of-$N$ selection; and (iii)~whether findings generalize across a difficulty gradient.\nOur Soft Jaccard signal addresses (i) through type-aware bipartite matching with token-level span overlap; our selection analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}) directly addresses (ii); and our three-dataset evaluation spanning easy (CoNLL) to hard (SciERC) tasks addresses (iii).\nCoCoA \\cite{vashurin2025cocoa} fuses model confidence with inter-sample consistency for QA/summarization but does not address structured extraction.\nOur evaluation builds on generative IE work \\cite{zhou2024uniner, sainz2024gollie, josifoski2022genie, li2023codeie, wadhwa2023revisiting, wang2023instructuie} and constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided}.\n\n\\paragraph{Uncertainty estimation and calibration.}\nSurveys cover confidence estimation \\cite{geng2024confidence}, selective abstention \\cite{wen2025know}, uncertainty quantification \\cite{xia2025survey}, and calibration \\cite{hobelsberger2025systematic} for LLMs; deep ensembles \\cite{lakshminarayanan2017ensembles} and MC-dropout \\cite{gal2016dropout} obtain uncertainty from model variation, while our approach requires no model modification.\nPrior NER uncertainty work spans Bayesian inference for sequence labeling \\cite{xiao2019uncertainty}, active learning under uncertainty \\cite{shelmanov2021active}, evidential deep learning \\cite{zhang2023ener}, verification-augmented NER \\cite{kim2024verifiner}, and uncertainty transmission across label sequences \\cite{he2024uncertainty}---all within discriminative paradigms.\nConformal prediction \\cite{fisch2021conformal, angelopoulos2021conformal} provides coverage guarantees; ```text
77	\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}
78	Filtering to non-zero F1 instances reshuffles rankings (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right).
79	EM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary), while VC and FK drop substantially (VC NER: $0.379{\to}0.300$; FK NER: $0.267{\to}0.176$).
80	LP RE collapses to $\rho{=}0.011$, confirming its full-set correlation was driven by zero/non-zero separation.
81	This creates a natural complementarity: VC and SJ are preferred on the full set, while EM provides the most precise ranking within the non-trivial subset (see Appendix~\ref{app:additional} for details).
82	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}\nFiltering to non-zero F1 instances reshuffles rankings (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right).\nEM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary), while VC and FK drop substantially (VC NER: $0.379{\\to}0.300$; FK NER: $0.267{\\to}0.176$).\nLP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$, confirming its full-set correlation was driven by zero/non-zero separation.\nThis creates a natural complementarity: VC and SJ are preferred on the full set, while EM provides the most precise ranking within the non-trivial subset (see Appendix~\\ref{app:additional} for details).\n",

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}\nFiltering to non-zero F1 instances reshuffles rankings (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right).\nEM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary), while VC and FK drop substantially (VC NER: $0.379{\\to}0.300$; FK NER: $0.267{\\to}0.176$).\nLP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$, confirming its full-set correlation was driven by zero/non-zero separation.\nThis creates a natural complementarity: VC and SJ are preferred on the full set, while EM provides the most precise ranking within the non-trivial subset (see Appendix~\\ref{app:additional} for details).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Conditional filtering.}\nFiltering to non-zero F1 instances separates binary success detection (can the model extract \\textit{anything}?) from quality gradation (among successful extractions, which are better?), but excludes the hardest instances where models fail completely---the conditional set is easier on average.\nThis reshuffles signal rankings (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, right).\nEM emerges as the strongest conditional signal on both NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary), while VC and FK drop substantially (VC NER: $0.379{\\to}0.300$; FK NER: $0.267{\\to}0.176$).\nLP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$, confirming its full-set correlation was driven by zero/non-zero separation.\nThis creates a natural complementarity: VC and SJ are preferred on the full set, while EM provides the most precise ranking within the non-trivial subset (see Appendix~\\ref{app:additional} for details)."
}
